=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given a permutation P=(P_1,P_2,\dots,P_N) of (1,2,\dots,N).
You want to satisfy P_i=i for all i=1,2,\dots,N by performing the following operation zero or more times:

- Choose an integer k such that 1 \leq k \leq N. If k \geq 2, sort the 1-st through (k-1)-th terms of P in ascending order. Then, if k \leq N-1, sort the (k+1)-th through N-th terms of P in ascending order.

It can be proved that under the constraints of this problem, it is possible to satisfy P_i=i for all i=1,2,\dots,N with a finite number of operations for any P. Find the minimum number of operations required.
You have T test cases to solve.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
T
\mathrm{case}_1
\vdots
\mathrm{case}_T

Each case is given in the following format:
N
P_1 P_2 \dots P_N

Output

Print T lines. The i-th line should contain the answer for the i-th test case.

Constraints


- 1 \leq T \leq 10^5
- 3 \leq N \leq 2 \times 10^5
- P is a permutation of (1,2,\dots,N).
- All input values are integers.
- The sum of N across the test cases in a single input is at most 2 \times 10^5.

Sample Input 1

3
5
2 1 3 5 4
3
1 2 3
7
3 2 1 7 5 6 4

Sample Output 1

1
0
2

For the first test case,

- 
Performing the operation with k=1 results in P becoming (2,1,3,4,5).

- 
Performing the operation with k=2 results in P becoming (2,1,3,4,5).

- 
Performing the operation with k=3 results in P becoming (1,2,3,4,5).

- 
Performing the operation with k=4 results in P becoming (1,2,3,5,4).

- 
Performing the operation with k=5 results in P becoming (1,2,3,5,4).


Specifically, performing the operation with k=3 results in P satisfying P_i=i for all i=1,2,\dots,5. Therefore, the minimum number of operations required is 1.
For the third test case, performing the operation with k=4 followed by k=3 results in P changing as (3,2,1,7,5,6,4) \rightarrow (1,2,3,7,4,5,6) \rightarrow (1,2,3,4,5,6,7).

```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Let's simulate operation effects more abstractly.

Since sorting ascending is idempotent and order-preserving by value. The operation O_k on a permutation can be decomposed: It rearranges elements within prefix and suffix to sorted order. This is equivalent to applying a permutation to positions that is the unique sorting permutation within each side. It is an involution? Is O_k an involution? If apply O_k twice, second time no change because after first, prefix and suffix sorted, so O_k fixes them. Thus O_k is idempotent, not involution generally (applying same k twice same as once). Sequence with distinct k? Repeating same no effect, so minimal uses distinct k? Could use same k after other operations maybe? Suppose O_a then O_b then O_a. Since O_a after O_b may change prefix/suffix around a, so second O_a could have effect. So same k can appear non-consecutively. But maybe minimal no repeats? Need not assume.

Operation O_k sorts all elements except P_k by their natural order within each side. This is like "stabilizing" around pivot k. It does not change the set on each side of k. Thus if we know the sequence of pivots, the final position of each element maybe determined by last operation that includes it? Let's analyze.

Given sequence of operations with pivots k_1,...,k_m. Consider an element v initially at position pos0. Each operation O_k:
- If current position pos = k, element stays at k.
- If pos < k, it is in prefix; after sorting prefix by value, its new position within prefix is its rank among values in prefix. This depends on set of elements in prefix, not just v.
- If pos > k, similarly.

This is complex but maybe can be characterized by "last time element is not pivot".

Maybe there is a monotonic invariant: The operation never changes the relative order of elements that are both on same side? It sorts them, so after operation their relative order is by value. Thus after at least one operation involving them, they become ordered.

Maybe we can think in terms of "bad positions" / "cycles".

Alternative perspective: Operation O_k sorts prefix and suffix. It is equivalent to:
- Let A = P[1..k-1], x=P[k], B=P[k+1..N].
- After operation, A becomes sorted(A), B becomes sorted(B).
Thus the only "unsortedness" remaining after O_k is between the three blocks: sorted(A), x, sorted(B). If sorted(A) and sorted(B) are globally in correct value ranges, then one more operation maybe.

Maybe we can view the process as gradually sorting by choosing pivots. Since sorting a segment ascending is easy; the challenge is moving elements across pivot positions. An element can cross a position k only when an operation with pivot not equal to that position includes it in a sorted side. For example, element at position k (pivot) can move in later operation with pivot j != k if j<k (then position k in suffix) or j>k (position k in prefix). Thus pivot positions are not barriers permanently.

But if prefix identity, sorting a sub-prefix before k (which is identity) no change; suffix after k includes positions k+1..N, may include identity positions k+1..r but sorting them may disturb if they are not sorted? Actually if positions k+1..r are identity, they are sorted ascending and contain correct values, but suffix also includes later elements; sorting whole suffix will rearrange identity prefix elements with others, so disturbs. So not monotonic.

B has three sorted blocks:
- Left block C sorted, size a-1, contains N (max).
- Middle x.
- Right block D sorted, size N-a, contains 1 (min).

Because N is in left block, any position in left block has value maybe up to N. Because 1 is in right block, any position in right block has value at least 1.

Can there be b with B_b=b and prefix set {1..b-1}? Let's analyze by location of b.

If b is in left block (position b <= a-1). Since left block sorted ascending and contains N. For B_b=b and prefix before b (within left block) to be {1..b-1}, the left block's first b elements must be exactly 1..b. But left block contains N at some position >? Since N is max, if N is within first b positions, then first b contains N > b, impossible. If N after position b, then first b elements are some b smallest elements of L. Could they be 1..b? But 1 is in right block, not L, so 1 cannot be in left block. Therefore first b cannot contain 1. Thus prefix before b cannot be {1..b-1} (which includes 1) unless b=1. If b=1, prefix empty, need B1=1. But B1 is first element of left block. Left block contains N and no 1 (1 in right), so B1 cannot be1? Could B1=1? No 1 not in L. So no. Thus b not in left block.

If b is middle position b=a. Need B_a=x=a. Prefix positions1..a-1 = left block set L must be {1..a-1}. But L contains N, and N > a-1, impossible. So no.

If b is in right block (position b > a). Right block sorted ascending and contains 1. Since b > a, prefix before b includes entire left block and middle and part of right block. For prefix set {1..b-1}, it must contain all values 1..b-1. In particular, since N > b-1 (unless b=N+1? b<=N, so N>=b? If b=N, N > b-1 yes), N cannot be in prefix. But N is in left block, which is entirely before b. Thus impossible. Therefore no valid b. Nice! This proves no 2 for bad endpoints. Good.

Condition for one operation with k:

Implementation:
import sys
def solve():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    it = iter(data)
    T = next(it)
    out = []
    for _ in range(T):
        N = next(it)
        P = [next(it) for _ in range(N)]
        # identity?
        ident = True
        one = False
        pref_max = 0
        for i, val in enumerate(P):
            if val != i+1:
                ident = False
            if val == i+1 and pref_max == i:
                one = True
            if val > pref_max:
                pref_max = val
        if ident:
            out.append('0')
        elif one:
            out.append('1')
        elif P[0] == N and P[-1] == 1:
            out.append('3')
        else:
            out.append('2')
    sys.stdout.write('\n'.join(out))
if __name__ == "__main__": solve()

Implementation with read:
def solve():
    import sys
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    idx=0
    T=data[idx]; idx+=1
    out=[]
    for _ in range(T):
        N=data[idx]; idx+=1
        P=data[idx:idx+N]; idx+=N
        ident=True
        one=False
        pref=0
        for i, v in enumerate(P):
            if v != i+1:
                ident=False
            if v == i+1 and pref == i:
                one=True
            if v > pref:
                pref = v
        if ident:
            out.append('0')
        elif one:
            out.append('1')
        elif P[0] == N and P[-1] == 1:
            out.append('3')
        else:
            out.append('2')
    sys.stdout.write('\n'.join(out))

Let's maybe prove no 2 for bad endpoints with a more concise argument:
After one operation O_a, if a=1 or a=N, the resulting array has no valid pivot. If 1<a<N, then position 1 (value N) lies in the left sorted block and position N (value 1) lies in the right sorted block. For a second operation with pivot b:
- If b is in left block, the prefix before b lacks 1 (unless b=1, but then B_1 !=1).
- If b is the middle, the prefix contains N.
- If b is in right block, the prefix contains N.
Thus no b can satisfy prefix set {1..b-1} and B_b=b. Good.

In the case b=a, prefix is left block set L, contains N > a-1, so cannot be {1..a-1}. Also B_a=P_a must equal a, but prefix condition already fails. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "P = data[idx:idx+N]" if N large and T many; total memory okay. If we want to avoid slice, we could process from data with start/end and store first,last, but need P for one-step scan? We can scan data directly:
start = idx
end = idx+N

    v = data[i]
    ...
Then P0=data[start], P_last=data[end-1]. This avoids slice. Might be slightly more memory efficient and avoids list allocation. Let's consider implementing this way:
data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
idx=0
T=data[idx]; idx+=1
out=[]
for _ in range(T):
    N=data[idx]; idx+=1
    start=idx
    end=idx+N
    idx=end
    ident=True
    one=False
    pref=0
    for i in range(start, end):
        v=data[i]
        pos=i-start
        if v != pos+1:
            ident=False
        if v == pos+1 and pref == pos:
            one=True
        if v > pref:
            pref = v
    if ident:
        out.append('0')
    elif one:
        out.append('1')
    elif data[start] == N and data[end-1] == 1:
        out.append('3')
    else:
        out.append('2')
This avoids P slice. But using P slice is simpler and less error-prone. Both fine. Since sum N 2e5, slice overhead negligible. Use P slice for clarity.

Let's maybe write final code:

```python
import sys

def solve():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    idx = 0
    T = data[idx]
    idx += 1
    ans = []
    for _ in range(T):
        N = data[idx]
        idx += 1
        P = data[idx:idx+N]
        idx += N

        ident = True
        one = False
        pref_max = 0

        for i, v in enumerate(P):
            if v != i + 1:
                ident = False
            if v == i + 1 and pref_max == i:
                one = True
            if v > pref_max:
                pref_max = v

        if ident:
            ans.append("0")
        elif one:
            ans.append("1")
        elif P[0] == N and P[-1] == 1:
            ans.append("3")
        else:
            ans.append("2")

    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(ans))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    solve()
```

This is final.

=== ANSWER ===
: The final answer should be only the Python code in a code block. I'll output that.</think>```python
import sys

def solve():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    idx = 0
    T = data[idx]
    idx += 1
    ans = []

    for _ in range(T):
        N = data[idx]
        idx += 1
        P = data[idx:idx + N]
        idx += N

        ident = True
        one = False
        pref_max = 0

        for i, v in enumerate(P):
            if v != i + 1:
                ident = False

            if v == i + 1 and pref_max == i:
                one = True

            if v > pref_max:
                pref_max = v

        if ident:
            ans.append("0")
        elif one:
            ans.append("1")
        elif P[0] == N and P[-1] == 1:
            ans.append("3")
        else:
            ans.append("2")

    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(ans))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    solve()
```
